# Strides: Habit Tracker + Goals (App Store ID 672401817) — Full Review Analysis

**App:** Strides: Habit Tracker + Goals — *Goal Planner & Daily Checklist*
**Developer:** Goals LLC (bundle `com.puresignal.strides`)
**Corpus:** 4,402 written App Store reviews, 90 storefronts, 22 July 2013 – 4 September 2026
**Extracted:** 2026-09-08T11:53:25Z · **Analysis date:** 12 September 2026
**Source:** `App Store Reviews/48. Strides - Habit Tracker + Goals - Goal Planner & Daily Checklist/`

---

## How to read this

Every number is a count of **written reviews in this corpus**, never of users, downloads or revenue. Themes are **non-exclusive**: one review can carry several, so theme percentages sum to more than 100%. The denominator is always stated. Signal labels follow the thresholds in `Store Review Analysis Prompt.md` (§ Signal thresholds) and are applied to the global corpus and to each eligible storefront separately.

Review IDs in backticks are exact `review_id` values from `reviews.jsonl`; §9.7 resolves every one of the 4,402 to its themes, and §9.8 resolves every theme to its reviews.

### ⚠️ Nine warnings before any number here is used

1. **This is a written-review corpus, not a rating corpus.** The App Store shows 4.8★ from ~19,000 ratings; the 4,402 people who also wrote text average **4.23★**. Writers skew to the strongly pleased and the strongly aggrieved.
2. **Most of the corpus predates the current app.** 59% of reviews (2,592) were written before 2019. The 2015 subscription crisis dominates the negative history but describes a product and price that no longer exist.
3. **Recency is thin.** The last 24 months (Oct 2024 – Sep 2026) hold **256 reviews, 5.8%** of the corpus. Every "current state" claim rests on that slice, not on the whole.
4. **The US is 56.45% of the corpus** (2,485). Global percentages are substantially US percentages. Nine storefronts clear the 50-review bar; 81 do not.
5. **No review states a price paid in a verifiable way.** Price complaints quote $4.99/mo, $29.99–$79.99/yr and $49.99–$149.99 lifetime across 11 years and many currencies. Prices are quoted as *reviewer claims*, dated, never as a price history.
6. **Payer identification is self-reported.** 172 reviews (3.91%) contain first-person evidence of paying. That is a floor on paid reviewers, not a conversion rate, and nothing here supports a conversion estimate.
7. **Free-tier cap numbers are inconsistent across reviewers.** Reviews variously claim 10, 8, 7, 6, 5, 4, 3, 2 and 1 free trackers, sometimes in the same month. The *direction* (tightening) is well evidenced; any single number is one reviewer's experience.
8. **Absence of a theme is not absence of the problem.** 416 reviews (9.45%) contain no product content at all ("Good", "👍", emoji). They are counted, never mined.
9. **Sentiment is inferred from text, not from stars.** 54 reviews rated 1–2★ contain genuine praise, and 37 reviews contradict their own star rating outright (`meta_rating_text_mismatch`); both are disclosed rather than dropped.

---

# EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

**Strides is an eleven-year-old, structurally loved, monetization-scarred product.** 72.38% of reviews (3,186) contain explicit praise of the product itself, at mean 4.84★. 14.04% (618) attack how it is sold, at mean 2.53★ — and those 618 reviews supply **51.0% of every 1–2★ review in the corpus**. Nothing in this corpus suggests users dislike Strides. A large, repeated minority dislikes the deal.

**1. The single most consequential event in the corpus is the January 2015 v3.0 conversion, and its lesson is still being paid for.** Strides began as a paid app. On 19 January 2015 it became a $4.99/month subscription requiring an account. In the nine months that followed (E2, n=308) the corpus mean fell to **2.21★ with 68.2% of reviews at 1–2★** — a collapse with no parallel elsewhere in this corpus. 92 reviews in that window describe the switch as a broken bargain: they had *bought* the app and were now asked to rent it (`mf_paid_then_subscription` 60; `mf_grandfather_broken` 14; `mf_forced_account_signup` 25). February 2015 alone ran **1.66★ mean, 83.9% 1–2★**. The developer reversed course: from October 2015 (E3) the corpus jumps to **4.62★** and monetization friction falls to 7.8%. The recovery is as clear as the crash. Both are in the same 12 months.

**2. The live monetization problem is not price — it is the free-tracker cap, and it is the only negative theme still growing.** `mf_free_cap_too_low` is 0.00% of E1, 1.59% of E3, 7.00% of E4 and **9.87% of E5 (2022+)**; in the last 24 months it is **12.1% of reviews**, the second-most-common theme of any kind behind generic praise. Counting reviewers who ask for a bigger free tier or object to it being cut, the union is **211 reviews (4.79%, very strong, mean 2.68★)**. Price complaints, by contrast, *fell* from 21.43% of E2 to 4.69% of E5. Users have stopped arguing that Strides is expensive and started arguing that they cannot evaluate it: 48 reviews say the limit was not disclosed before they invested setup effort (`mf_paywall_not_disclosed`, mean **1.65★**), and 16 say the cap shrank under them after they had built history on it (`mf_free_cap_reduced`).

**3. Reliability was an existential problem twice, and is now genuinely fixed.** Crash-family reviews (`rel_crash_wont_open` + `rel_crash_on_action` + `rel_frozen_unresponsive`) run 76 in E1 and 115 in E2 — **29.55% of E2 could not open the app at all** — then fall to 31 in E3 and **24 in E5 (2.89%)**. The whole Reliability family is 32.2% of E1, 52.6% of E2, and **8.3% of E5**. This is the clearest improvement in the corpus and it should be defended, not revisited.

**4. What users love is specific and unusual, and it is the moat.** The top praise themes are not "nice app": they are **flexible tracker types** (485, 11.02%, mean 4.82★ — habit/target/average/project, "not just yes/no"), **simplicity** (817, 18.56%), **best of many tried** (453, 10.29%, mean 4.89★ — reviewers who explicitly benchmarked rivals and returned), and **behaviour change actually achieved** (267, 6.07%, mean 4.95★ — weight lost, smoking quit, 1,000-day flossing streaks). 137 reviews identify as multi-year users, rising from 1.41% of E3 to **8.54% of E5**. Strides' retained base is its strongest asset and its loudest cap-complainers are often the same people.

**5. Paying customers are visible, satisfied and badly served when something breaks.** 172 reviews show first-person purchase evidence (3.91%), mean 3.92★ — *below* the corpus mean, because 84 of them (48.8% of payers) also report a problem. 73 reviews say the paid tier is worth it (mean 4.97★) and 23 name a lifetime purchase. But 23 payers demand refunds, 12 report billing faults, 8 got no support reply, and support is only reachable inside an app that 9 payers say will not open (`sup_only_reachable_in_app`, rising to 1.08% of E5). The paid experience fails at exactly the moments it matters.

**6. Support is a genuine differentiator, named person and all.** 127 reviews (2.89%, mean 4.89★) praise support responsiveness, very often naming the CEO by first name, and 55 confirm a reported bug was actually fixed. This is rare in a 4,402-review corpus and it partially offsets pricing anger — several reviewers upgraded their rating explicitly after contact.

**7. The 2021 logging redesign is the one self-inflicted UX wound that persists.** A mid-2021 change to history logging drew 10 reviews in three months complaining it took several times as many taps (`ux_too_many_taps` peaks at 1.09% of E4), from long-term users and payers, mean 3.86★ in that window. It was partially rolled back after user pressure — one reviewer documents the developer restoring the behaviour — but it cost ratings from the most loyal cohort.

**The strategic read:** Strides has solved its reliability problem and retained a devoted, outcome-reporting base. Its remaining growth constraint is that the free tier is now too small to demonstrate the product's one genuine differentiator — multi-type tracking across several goals — to people who have not yet paid. The corpus contains an unusually direct statement of the fix from users themselves: raise the trial surface, disclose it up front, and stop shrinking it retroactively.

# PART 1 — SCOPE AND METHOD

## 1.1 Files used

| File | Role |
|---|---|
| `App Store Reviews/48. .../reviews.jsonl` | Primary corpus — 4,402 records, one JSON object per line |
| `App Store Reviews/48. .../by_country/*.jsonl` | 90 per-storefront files, 4,402 records total |
| `App Store Reviews/48. .../manifest.json` | App metadata, per-country counts, rating distribution |
| `App Store Reviews/48. .../_state.json` | Per-storefront collection cursors and completion flags |

Working artifacts (gitignored, `Temp/`): `48-dump.py`, `48-reconcile.py`, `48-themes.py`, `48-idx-classification.py`, `48-build-classification.py`, `48-review-classification.py`, `48-validate.py`, `48-common.py`, `48-aggregate.py`, `48-trends.py`, `48-tables.py`, `48-build-report.py`, `48-verify-report.py`.

## 1.2 Schema

Every record carries exactly these 13 fields, with **no missing or empty values anywhere in the 4,402**:

`review_id`, `app_id`, `app_name`, `country`, `country_name`, `rating`, `title`, `body`, `author`, `date`, `vote_count`, `vote_sum`, `is_edited`.

Fields used in this analysis: `review_id` (identity), `country`/`country_name` (market), `rating` (1–5), `title` + `body` (classification), `date` (trend), `is_edited` and `vote_count`/`vote_sum` (integrity checks). `author` was read during classification but is not reported: no reviewer is named in this document. `app_id` and `app_name` are constant.

HTML entities in `title`/`body` (`&#39;`, `&amp;`, `&quot;`) were unescaped for reading and classification; raw values in `reviews.jsonl` are unchanged.

## 1.3 Coverage and reconciliation

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Records in `reviews.jsonl` | **4,402** |
| Unique `review_id` values | **4,402** (zero duplicates) |
| Records across the 90 `by_country/*.jsonl` files | **4,402** |
| Country-file IDs absent from main file | **0** |
| Main-file IDs absent from country files | **0** |
| Country-file records not identical to main-file records | **0** |
| Records whose `country` disagrees with their filename | **0** |
| Per-country counts, country files vs main file | **identical for all 90** |
| `manifest.json` `total_reviews` vs actual | 4,402 vs **4,402** ✓ |
| `manifest.json` `storefronts_with_reviews` vs observed | 90 vs **90** ✓ |
| `manifest.json` `rating_distribution` vs actual | 5★ 3057 / 4★ 450 / 3★ 214 / 2★ 197 / 1★ 484 — **exact match** ✓ |
| `manifest.json` `mean_rating` vs actual | 4.226 vs **4.226** ✓ |
| `manifest.json` `reviews_per_country` vs actual | **exact match for all 90** ✓ |
| `_state.json` | all 145 listed storefronts are `complete: true`; 55 of them returned zero written reviews and 90 returned at least one ✓ |
| `_state.json` `collected` vs per-country actuals | **matches for every storefront** ✓ |
| Duplicate (`author`, `date`) pairs | **0** |
| **Reviews read individually** | **4,402 (100%)** |
| **Reviews classified** | **4,402 (100%)** — zero unassigned, zero unknown IDs, zero empty theme lists |

**No deduplication was performed.** Two pairs of near-identical reviews exist and are flagged rather than merged (`meta_duplicate_text`, 3 reviews): `10499805369`/`10499821074` (Uruguay, same reviewer text posted twice, Oct 2023) and `12631350374`/`12631366285` (US, same text, May 2025). Each remains a separate record in every denominator; the effect on any percentage is under 0.07pp.

## 1.4 Date range and shape

First review `840926526` (Australia, 5★, 22 July 2013). Last review `14509426891` (New Zealand, 1★, 4 September 2026). Span: **13 years, 1.5 months**.

| Year | n | % of corpus | Mean ★ | 5★ | 4★ | 3★ | 2★ | 1★ | 1–2★ share | US |

| 2016 | 573 | 13.0% | 4.63 | 483 | 37 | 13 | 12 | 28 | 7.0% | 331 |
| 2017 | 595 | 13.5% | 4.62 | 484 | 56 | 18 | 13 | 24 | 6.2% | 350 |
| 2018 | 716 | 16.3% | 4.65 | 573 | 89 | 23 | 8 | 23 | 4.3% | 424 |

| 2020 | 377 | 8.6% | 4.18 | 247 | 48 | 23 | 19 | 40 | 15.6% | 207 |

Body length: median **117 characters**, mean 177, minimum 1 (`11001358061`), maximum 4,350 (`1647723891`, US, 3★). 382 reviews have a body of 20 characters or fewer.

**January dominates.** 775 reviews (17.6%) were written in January — 2.3× the average month — consistent with New Year resolution behaviour for a goal-tracking app. The single largest month is January 2018 (144). The second is January 2015 (125), which is the subscription crisis rather than resolution season.

**Volume is declining.** 2018 peaked at 716 written reviews; 2025 saw 138 and 2026 is on pace below that. This reduces statistical power for recent findings and is the main reason §7 leans on rates rather than counts for E5.

## 1.5 Processing method

1. **Parse and profile** (`48-dump.py`): load all 4,402 records, unescape HTML entities, sort by (`date`, `review_id`), emit a single working file plus 45 hundred-review chunks.
2. **Read every review individually** in chunk order — all 4,402, in their original languages (English, Spanish, Portuguese, German, Russian, French, Italian, Dutch, Swedish, Danish, Norwegian, Finnish, Polish, Czech, Slovak, Turkish, Hungarian, Croatian, Lithuanian, Simplified and Traditional Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Thai, Vietnamese, Indonesian, Arabic, Ukrainian, Catalan). No sampling, no keyword filtering, no truncation at a context limit.
3. **Hand-curate the taxonomy** (`48-themes.py`): 117 themes in 11 families, defined from what the corpus actually says rather than from a prior list.
4. **Hand-classify by working-file index** (`48-idx-classification.py`): each of the 4,402 reviews mapped to its themes while reading. Keying by index rather than by 11-digit ID removes transcription risk; `48-build-classification.py` resolves index → `review_id` deterministically from `reviews.jsonl` using the same sort as step 1, producing `48-review-classification.py`.
5. **Validate** (`48-validate.py`): all checks pass — 4,402 source records, 4,402 classified, 0 duplicate keys, 0 unknown IDs, 0 unassigned records, 0 empty theme lists, 0 intra-review duplicate themes, 0 unknown theme names, 0 orphan themes. 8,804 theme assignments across 4,402 reviews (mean 2.00 per review).
6. **Aggregate and trend** (`48-aggregate.py`, `48-trends.py`): counts, rating bands, payer segment, storefronts, market groups, eras, years, incident windows.
7. **Generate every table mechanically** (`48-tables.py`) and assemble the report from source + generated tables (`48-build-report.py`), which refuses to write if any placeholder is unfilled or any count check fails.
8. **Verify after writing** (`48-verify-report.py`): every review ID cited exists in `reviews.jsonl`; every §9.7 index row matches the map and the source record; every §3.1 master-table row matches recomputed counts; every §9.8 ID list matches the map.

**Classification validation.** Classification is manual, so the error mode is judgement, not parsing. Three guards: every theme is defined by an explicit assignment rule (§9.1); borderline families (`mf_price_too_high` vs `mf_free_cap_too_low`; `cp_simplicity` vs `cp_generic_positive`; `rel_crash_wont_open` vs `rel_frozen_unresponsive`) were given disjoint rules before classification and re-checked after; and all 117 themes are used, so no theme is an untested category. Residual risk is highest on short reviews where intent is genuinely ambiguous — those default to `cp_generic_positive` or `meta_low_information` rather than to a substantive theme, which biases *against* overstating findings.

## 1.6 Limitations and known biases

- **Self-selection.** Written reviews over-represent strong feeling. The 4.23★ written mean sits below the 4.8★ all-ratings average reported on the store listing (accessed 12 Sep 2026).
- **Prompted reviews.** 3 reviews explicitly mention being asked to review; 2 more complain the prompt appears too early (`ux_review_prompt_nag`, 7 total). An in-app prompt existed at least in 2013–2015 and again around 2021. Some share of the 379 five-star `meta_low_information` reviews is prompt-driven; that share is unknowable from this data.
- **No burst or brigading pattern was found.** The January 2015 spike (125 reviews) is a genuine product event, not a coordinated campaign: it spans 20 storefronts, its texts are unrelated, and it tracks a documented version change. No other month shows anomalous clustering.
- **Votes are near-absent.** Only 314 reviews (7.1%) carry any helpfulness vote; the maximum is 36. Votes are not used to weight anything.
- **42 reviews are edited** (`is_edited`), several visibly rewritten after developer contact (e.g. `1811801602`, `1832...`, `9993...`). They are classified on final text; earlier text is not recoverable.
- **Prices are reviewer claims.** No price is verified against a historical price list, because none is available in the corpus. Current App Store IAP tiers are cited separately in §2.3 with their access date.
- **Storefront ≠ nationality or language.** `country` is the App Store storefront. A review in Russian may sit in `de`; several do.
- **Sub-50 storefronts carry no standalone claims.** 81 of 90 storefronts have under 50 reviews. Their reviews are in every global number; they get no independent conclusions (§6.7).
- **Dates are review-publication dates**, not purchase or first-use dates. A 2026 review may describe 2015 behaviour, and several do — era assignment reflects when the review was *written*.

# PART 2 — PRODUCT AND MONETIZATION MODEL

## 2.1 Feature inventory derived from reviews

Everything below is attested by reviewers in this corpus. Counts are reviews that praise, request or report a fault in that capability — a measure of *salience in reviews*, not of usage.

| Capability | What reviewers say it does | Evidence (reviews) |
|---|---|---|
| **Four tracker types** — Habit (yes/no), Target (a number by a date), Average (rolling mean), Project (milestones, %) | The defining feature. "Not just the binary yes/no of traditional habit apps" | 485 praise `cp_flexible_tracker_types`; `917379605` `1370794562` `1891448103` `11461372218` `13646840535` |
| **Bad-habit / "less is better" tracking** | Track a limit, not a goal; red/green inverts | 105 praise `cp_bad_habit_tracking`; `6553635234` (quit smoking, benchmarked 10+ rivals) |
| **Streaks and goal streaks** | Configurable streak targets; "don't break the chain" | 144 `cp_streak_motivation`; `11672096169` (507-day streak), `13989739905` (1,000 consecutive days, 3,497 tracked) |
| **Multiple custom reminders per tracker, with user-written text** | Several alerts/day; the message is the reviewer's own words | 224 `cp_reminders`; `3092522796` `3026979984` |
| **Dashboard, charts, calendar, pace line** | Progress bars, monthly calendar, "where you should be today" | 241 `cp_visual_progress`; 56 `cp_goal_pacing` |
| **Notes on each log** | Per-entry journalling | 14 request, some praise `cp_notes_journal` |
| **Tags / filters / Today list** | Grouping and filtering (premium) | 26 request `fr_tags_categories` |
| **Reports / trends** | Added over time; still the top open request | 76 `fr_better_reports` |
| **Apple Watch app and complication** | Praised when working, faulted when blank | 24 `cp_watch_app`; 12 `rel_watch_bug`; 5 `fr_watch_complication` |
| **Home-screen / lock-screen widgets** | Arrived ~2020; interactivity later removed | 12 `cp_widget`; 20 `fr_widget`; 14 `rel_widget_bug` |
| **Apple Health integration and Siri Shortcuts** | Auto-log steps/weight; voice logging | 17 `cp_health_shortcuts`; 3 `rel_health_sync_bug` |
| **Mac app** (from ~March 2022) | Syncs with iOS; welcomed by long-term users | `8407086509` `8409343866` `9053449079` |
| **Web app** (2015 – ~2019, discontinued) | Once a headline feature; its removal is still resented | 69 `cp_multi_platform_sync`; 17 `fr_web_or_other_platform` |
| **CSV export** | Premium; praised and occasionally broken | 16 `fr_backup_export`; 4 `rel_export_bug` |
| **Goal-setting course / handbook** | Free companion content, repeatedly credited | 27 `seg_book_or_course`; `12948472060` `14500682632` |
| **iCloud sync across devices** | Replaced the account-based sync of 2015 | 48 `rel_sync_fail` when it misbehaves |

Capabilities reviewers **ask for and do not have**: shared/partner accountability (22), true time-duration units HH:MM (8), an API or IFTTT/Zapier automation (9), a built-in timer (5), Android (within `fr_web_or_other_platform`), skip/vacation mode (13 `ux_no_skip_or_pause`), arbitrary cadences such as every-other-day (35 `fr_flexible_frequency`).

## 2.2 Free / paid classification

The corpus describes **four distinct monetization regimes** over 11 years. This is the single most important structural fact about Strides' reviews.

| Era | Model as reviewers describe it | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| **2013 – Jan 2015** | **Paid app**, roughly $2.99–$4.99 one-time. No account, no subscription. | `926228901` ("not really worth $2.99"), `974433082` ("well worth $4.00"), `1097369177` ("£2.49") |
| **Jan – Sep 2015** | **Subscription-only**, $4.99/mo or $49.99/yr, **account required**, 30-day trial. Prior purchasers told they were grandfathered; many say they were not. | `1132627055` `1133263942` `1136026653` `1152141697` `1153567265` |
| **Oct 2015 – mid 2018** | **Freemium relaunch.** A generous free tier (reviewers say ~10 trackers) with Plus adding tags, filters, notes, export, unlimited trackers. | `1273838707` (announces "free unlimited habits"), `1311552630` `1999423881` |
| **mid 2018 – present** | **Freemium with a tightening cap**: reviewers report 10 → 7 (mid-2018) → lower still (3 is the most-cited number from 2020 on). Plus is monthly/annual/lifetime. | `2957558274` ("says forever free with 10 goals… u only get 7"), `3168367859`, `6021145901`, `12401006422` |

**Free vs paid, as attested by reviewers (current era):**

| Tier | Contains | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| **Free** | A small fixed number of trackers (most commonly reported as 3), all four tracker types, unlimited custom reminders, calendar/history, streaks, widgets, Watch app, no ads | 248 `mp_free_enough`; 33 `cp_no_ads` |
| **Plus (paid)** | Unlimited trackers, tags/filters/Today list, notes on goals, data export, cross-device sync, reports | `mf_free_cap_too_low` 199 name the tracker cap as the paywall; `5435794858` and `6378721003` name the web app and export |
| **Unclear from reviews** | Which specific report views are gated; whether notes are free (one reviewer and the developer disagree in `1647723891`) | flagged, not resolved |

**Ads:** none, in any era. 33 reviews praise the absence of ads; **zero reviews complain about ads**. Two reviewers propose ads *instead of* the paywall (`8005170579`, `7555174284`). This is a deliberate and well-received position.

## 2.3 External sources

Used only to describe the product's current commercial shape, kept separate from review-derived evidence:

- **App Store listing for Strides (ID 672401817)**, accessed **12 September 2026**: free download with In-App Purchases; "Strides Plus" tiers listed at **$4.99, $7.99, $9.99, $29.99, $39.99, $99.99 and $149.99**; current version **19.13** (released 16 August 2025); rating **4.8 from ~19,000 ratings**; category Productivity; minimum **iOS 17.0**; 10 listed languages (English, French, German, Japanese, Korean, Portuguese, Russian, Simplified/Traditional Chinese, Spanish). The listing states the free tier includes **3 trackers**, with Plus adding unlimited trackers, sync, tags/filters, Today list, export and goal notes.
- **stridesapp.com**, accessed **12 September 2026**: confirms the four tracker types (Habit, Target, Average, Project) and iPhone/iPad/Mac support, and advertises an **Android waitlist**. It publishes no prices, no free-tier limit and no mention of the discontinued web app.

Two facts worth holding together: the store's **3-tracker** free tier matches what reviewers have reported since roughly 2020, and the **~19,000 ratings vs 4,402 written reviews** ratio means this corpus is the vocal 23%.

## 3.2 Theme-family aggregates

Union of all themes in the family — a review counts once per family however many of its themes it carries.

| Family | Themes | Reviews (union) | % of 4,402 | Mean ★ | Share of the 681 1–2★ reviews | Signal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Core praise | 24 | 3186 | 72.38% | 4.84 | 44/681 (6.5%) | **high-priority** |
| Monetization friction | 16 | 618 | 14.04% | 2.53 | 347/681 (51.0%) | **high-priority** |
| Reliability | 17 | 583 | 13.24% | 2.40 | 337/681 (49.5%) | **high-priority** |
| Meta | 4 | 454 | 10.31% | 4.79 | 15/681 (2.2%) | **high-priority** |
| Segments | 10 | 395 | 8.97% | 4.65 | 27/681 (4.0%) | **high-priority** |
| Monetization praise | 4 | 351 | 7.97% | 4.77 | 11/681 (1.6%) | **high-priority** |
| UX friction | 14 | 346 | 7.86% | 3.35 | 107/681 (15.7%) | **high-priority** |
| Feature gaps & requests | 19 | 321 | 7.29% | 4.07 | 39/681 (5.7%) | **high-priority** |
| Product-value criticism | 4 | 250 | 5.68% | 2.28 | 166/681 (24.4%) | **high-priority** |
| Purchase evidence | 2 | 172 | 3.91% | 3.92 | 41/681 (6.0%) | **very strong** |
| Support | 3 | 95 | 2.16% | 3.43 | 34/681 (5.0%) | meaningful |
| **Any praise** (core + monetization praise + fix acknowledged) | — | **3296** | **74.88%** | **4.82** | 54/681 (7.9%) | **high-priority** |

Read the last two columns together. **Core praise is 72.38% of the corpus but supplies only 6.5% of its 1–2★ reviews. Monetization friction is 14.04% of the corpus and supplies 51.0% of them.** Reliability, once equally destructive, supplies 49.5% — but §7 shows that damage is overwhelmingly historical, while the monetization damage is current.

## 3.3 Positive themes in detail

### 3.3.1 Simplicity — 817 reviews (18.56%, **high-priority**, mean 4.84★)

The most-cited concrete virtue, stable across every era (16.08% of E1, 21.52% of E3, 19.86% of E5). Reviewers mean two different things and both matter: *fast to log* ("open the app, click a check mark, exit") and *uncluttered to look at*. It coexists with the app's depth rather than contradicting it — `2388561708`'s "the most robust habit tracker without being bloated" is the corpus's own summary. IDs: `840926526` `922451377` `1124728275` `2272547310` `6127536025` `9867363272` `13646840535`.

### 3.3.2 Flexible tracker types — 485 reviews (11.02%, **high-priority**, mean 4.82★)

**This is the differentiator.** Reviewers describe arriving from apps that only offer yes/no and staying because Strides tracks numbers, averages, targets and projects. `917379605` (2013) states the thesis — "I don't want to reduce everything to the binary yes/no" — and `11461372218` (2024) repeats it verbatim in structure eleven years later. `1204359342` lists seven rival apps tried before concluding "Strides is the only app that does both". Highest in GB (13.79%), AU (14.52%), IN (16.90%). This theme, not streaks or design, is why people switch *to* Strides.

### 3.3.3 "Best of the many I tried" — 453 reviews (10.29%, **high-priority**, mean 4.89★)

Explicit competitive benchmarking, often naming rivals. Sustained: 203 in E3, 132 in E4, 80 in E5. When these reviewers name what won, it is almost always tracker flexibility plus simplicity. IDs: `954271910` `1002953773` `2540082589` `6442694958` `12143126487` `14210458077`.

### 3.3.4 Behaviour change actually achieved — 267 reviews (6.07%, **high-priority**, mean 4.95★)

The highest-mean substantial theme in the corpus. These are outcome claims, not feature praise: 80 lbs lost (`10643597772`), 31 lbs and 26 inches (`9461414147`), quit smoking (`1529693362`, `1629695797`), 1,000 consecutive days of flossing across a 3,497-day tracking history (`13989739905`), a business built (`9693465813`), a degree finished (`5297074809`). Rising: 3.51% of E1 → 6.17% of E3 → **8.18% of E5**. US over-indexes (+1.5pp).

### 3.3.5 Streaks, visual progress and pacing — 144 / 241 / 56 reviews

`cp_streak_motivation` (144, mean **4.96★** — the highest of any theme with n>100) is the purest motivation mechanic: "I can't bear to see it go". `cp_visual_progress` (241) is the charts, calendar and red/green. `cp_goal_pacing` (56) is the quietly distinctive one — the projected-date and "am I on pace" maths that only the Target and Project types provide (`1630702511`, `6790034274`, `13917984452`).

### 3.3.6 Support and developer responsiveness — 127 + 103 reviews (2.89% + 2.34%)

127 reviews praise support (mean 4.89★), frequently naming the CEO; 103 praise shipped updates and the public feature-voting board; 55 confirm a specific fix (`sup_fix_acknowledged`, mean 4.78★). Several reviewers raise their rating in-place after contact (`1811801602`, `1957498454`, `2858976575`, `7540012549`). US (3.38%) and GB (3.83%) over-index. In an 11-year corpus this is an unusually durable asset.

### 3.3.7 Monetization praise — 351 reviews (7.97%, mean 4.77★)

`mp_free_enough` (248) is the load-bearing one: reviewers who say the free tier suffices, overwhelmingly from E3–E4 when it was large (7.82% of E3, 4.81% of E5). `mp_worth_the_price` (73, mean 4.97★) and `mp_pay_to_support_dev` (9) are the paid counterweight. Note the asymmetry with §3.4.1: free-tier satisfaction is **falling** as the cap tightens while cap complaints rise.

## 3.4 Negative themes in detail

### 3.4.1 The free-tracker cap — 211 reviews (4.79%, **very strong**, mean 2.68★) — *the live problem*

The union of `mf_free_cap_too_low` (199), `mf_free_cap_reduced` (23) and `fr_raise_free_cap` (15). Trajectory: **0 in E1, 1 in E2, 28 in E3, 97 in E4, 85 in E5** — and 32 in the last 24 months alone, where it reaches **12.1% of all reviews**. It is the only major negative theme whose rate is still climbing.

Three distinct complaints sit inside it, and they need different fixes:

1. **The cap is too small to evaluate the product** (199). "No one has just one habit they need to track" (`12919506637`); "I can't do much with just three free task" (`5260457163`); "Statistics may state you should only focus on a couple habits but you shouldn't make people feel like they can't have more than 3 unless they pay" (`8219828649`, a long, constructive 1★).
2. **The cap shrank retroactively** (23). Reviewers who built history on 10 or 7 trackers found themselves unable to re-add one after deleting it: `2946161512`, `2957558274`, `3168367859`, `4872544084`, `6652736923`, `12980575138`. This reads as punishment of existing users, and its mean is **2.22★**.
3. **The limit was not disclosed before setup effort** (48 `mf_paywall_not_disclosed`, mean **1.65★**). "I lost my time encoding 3 trackers for nothing" (`8794696710`); "Wish i knew before i downloaded and set everything up" (`11069487020`). Rising: 0.49% of E3 → **2.05% of E5**.

### 3.4.2 Price — 316 reviews (7.18%, **high-priority**, mean 2.74★) — *falling, not rising*

Union of `mf_price_too_high` (266), `mf_want_one_time_purchase` (58), `mf_subscription_objection` (48) and `mf_lifetime_price_too_high` (28). The rate **fell** from 21.43% of E2 to 4.69% of E5. Two things drive what remains: the subscription *model* ("I won't rent an app"), and the lifetime price, quoted by reviewers at $79.99–$149.99 and repeatedly called disproportionate for a tracker (`5466637763`, `7589358605`, `9942354598`, `13205964615`). Notably, 73 reviewers say the opposite — that it is worth it — at mean 4.97★.

### 3.4.3 The 2015 subscription conversion — 115 reviews (2.61%, mean 1.57★) — *historical but instructive*

Union of `mf_paid_then_subscription` (70), `mf_forced_account_signup` (39) and `mf_grandfather_broken` (14). **92 of the 115 fall in E2**; the theme is essentially extinct after 2016 (3 reviews in E5, all from users returning after years away). Its texts are the angriest in the corpus: "bait and switch" (`1190739692`), "SCAM" (`1217176354`, `1256067864`), "Rent what you've paid for" (`1252178949`). 25 reviews object specifically to being forced to create an account and go online for a local tracker — a privacy objection, distinct from price (`1136681651`, `1540297507`, `2113118458`).

### 3.4.4 Reliability — 583 reviews (13.24%, mean 2.40★) — *solved, and worth defending*

| Sub-theme | n | Mean ★ | E1 | E2 | E3 | E4 | E5 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `rel_crash_wont_open` | 185 | 1.88 | 41 | 91 | 17 | 22 | 14 |
| `rel_crash_on_action` | 82 | 2.62 | 35 | 18 | 11 | 9 | 9 |
| `rel_wrong_counts_stats` | 85 | 2.59 | 22 | 8 | 26 | 20 | 9 |
| `rel_notification_bug` | 78 | 2.64 | 17 | 15 | 20 | 17 | 9 |
| `rel_data_loss` | 58 | 1.67 | 7 | 25 | 9 | 13 | 4 |
| `rel_sync_fail` | 48 | 2.65 | 0 | 11 | 12 | 12 | 13 |
| `rel_frozen_unresponsive` | 41 | 2.15 | 3 | 9 | 3 | 25 | 1 |
| `rel_duplicate_entries` | 32 | 1.72 | 1 | 20 | 4 | 5 | 2 |

Two crises: an **iOS 8 / iPhone 6 era** (late 2014, crash-on-adding-a-tracker and crash-on-back-button — 20 reviews in the Sep–Dec 2014 window) and the **v3.0 sync rewrite** (2015: crash-on-launch at 29.55% of E2, plus duplicated trackers and lost data). Both were fixed. The Reliability family is **8.3% of E5** and the crash union is 24 reviews (2.89%) there. The one sub-theme that has *not* fallen is `rel_sync_fail` — flat at 11–13 reviews per era since 2015, now the most common reliability complaint in E5.

### 3.4.5 UX friction — 346 reviews (7.86%, mean 3.35★)

Led by `ux_confusing_setup` (118, mean 3.11★) — steady at 2–3% in every era except E2, and the corpus's most persistent usability complaint. Reviewers cannot work out how to configure a goal type, how to log a number, or where deletion lives. `ux_yes_no_semantics` (12) is its sharpest form: bad-habit logging where a tap's meaning is genuinely ambiguous (`7506344777`, `9016389877`, `12374920319`). `ux_too_many_taps` (31) spikes in mid-2021 with the history-logging redesign. `ux_no_skip_or_pause` (13) and `ux_weekday_scheduling` (21) are the two structural scoring complaints: a weekly goal still demands a daily answer, and there is no vacation mode. `ux_dated_ui` (62) rises slowly and is now 1.68% of E5.

### 3.4.6 Support failure — 40 reviews (0.91%, mean 1.57★)

`sup_no_response` (29) and `sup_only_reachable_in_app` (14). The second is a structural trap and it is **growing** (1.08% of E5): the only support route is inside the app, so the users who most need help — those whose app will not launch — cannot reach it. Nine such reviews are explicitly 1★ reviews written *as* support tickets (`9696594665`, `10162216990`, `11302947469`, `13576643207`, `12211893087`).

## 3.5 Unmet needs — requests, broken features, misunderstandings and price objections kept apart

The spec requires these four to be distinguished. They are separate families here:

- **Genuine new-capability requests** (`fr_*`, 321 reviews, 7.29%, mean 4.07★ — note the *high* mean: these come from satisfied users). Top: better reports/trends (76), flexible cadences (35), tags/categories (26), end-dates and archiving (23), shared accountability (22), widgets (20), web/Android (17), gamified rewards (17), export/backup (16), raise the free cap (15), notes (14), units (13), timer (5), time-duration HH:MM (8), API (9), dark mode (11).
- **Broken existing capabilities** (`rel_*`, 583) — §3.4.4. Distinct from requests: the feature exists and fails.
- **Misunderstandings** (`ux_confusing_setup`, 118) — the capability exists and the reviewer could not find or interpret it. Several are resolved in-thread by support, which is why this theme's mean (3.11★) sits above the reliability themes.
- **Pricing objections** (`mf_*`, 618) — §3.4.1–3.4.3. Never counted as feature gaps.

The most valuable request signal is not the largest count but the pairing: **`fr_better_reports` (76, mean 4.07★, still open in Aug 2026) comes almost entirely from people who like the app**, and `fr_social_accountability` (22, mean 4.45★) includes a coach who says he would move 100 clients onto Strides if it existed (`8597913328`).

---

# PART 4 — RATINGS ANALYSIS

Distribution: **5★ 3,057 (69.45%) · 4★ 450 (10.22%) · 3★ 214 (4.86%) · 2★ 197 (4.48%) · 1★ 484 (11.00%)**. Mean **4.23**. The shape is strongly bimodal — 80% of reviews sit at the extremes — which is why the mean alone conceals both the loyalty and the anger in this corpus.

## 4.1 Five stars — n = 3,057 (69.45%)

Top themes: `cp_generic_positive` 783 (25.6%), `cp_simplicity` 726 (23.7%), `cp_flexible_tracker_types` 420 (13.7%), `cp_best_of_many` 415 (13.6%), `meta_low_information` 379 (12.4%), `cp_behaviour_change` 256 (8.4%), `mp_free_enough` 216 (7.1%), `cp_visual_progress` 213 (7.0%), `cp_reminders` 198 (6.5%).

Two observations. First, **12.4% of five-star reviews contain no product content at all** — "Good", "👍", a single emoji — so the effective evidentiary five-star base is nearer 2,680. Second, 89 five-star reviews are from self-identified payers and 216 from people saying the *free* tier suffices: the five-star band is not a paid band.

## 4.2 Four stars — n = 450 (10.22%)

The "yes, but" band, and the most diagnostically useful. `mf_price_too_high` appears in 38 (8.4%) and `mf_free_cap_too_low` in 28 (6.2%) — **pricing is the single largest reason a satisfied user withholds the fifth star**. Then `ux_confusing_setup` 22 (4.9%) and `fr_better_reports` 22 (4.9%). A four-star review here typically reads: the product is right, the deal or the polish is not.

## 4.3 Three stars — n = 214 (4.86%)

Now inverted — negatives lead. `mf_price_too_high` 43 (20.1%), `mf_free_cap_too_low` 33 (15.4%), then a reliability cluster: `rel_notification_bug` 21, `rel_wrong_counts_stats` 21, `rel_crash_on_action` 20. `neg_stated_churn` appears in 18 (8.4%): at three stars, people are already announcing departure.

## 4.4 Two stars — n = 197 (4.48%)

`mf_price_too_high` 43 (21.8%), `mf_free_cap_too_low` 35 (17.8%), `rel_crash_wont_open` 30 (15.2%), `neg_stated_churn` 24 (12.2%), `neg_competitor_named` 23 (11.7%). The two-star band is where pricing anger and a named competitor arrive together — these reviewers have done comparison shopping and lost.

## 4.5 One star — n = 484 (11.00%)

`rel_crash_wont_open` 113 (23.3%), `mf_price_too_high` 87 (18.0%), `mf_free_cap_too_low` 67 (13.8%), `neg_stated_churn` 57 (11.8%), `mf_paid_then_subscription` 52 (10.7%), `neg_not_worth_it` 37 (7.6%), `rel_data_loss` 36 (7.4%), `mf_paywall_not_disclosed` 30 (6.2%), `mf_forced_account_signup` 27 (5.6%), `mf_refund_request` 22 (4.5%), `sup_no_response` 21 (4.3%).

**Two causes own the one-star band and they are separable in time.** Of 484 one-star reviews, 214 fall in 2014–2015 (the crash era and the subscription conversion) and 270 are spread across the other eleven years. The crash cause is spent; the monetization cause is not. **31 one-star reviews are from self-identified payers** — the most expensive failure mode in the corpus.

## 4.7 Where stars and text disagree

37 reviews contradict their own rating (`meta_rating_text_mismatch`) — 5★ text describing a fatal bug, 1★ text that is pure praise. Examples: `922481870` (5★, "What happened? … totally fails to deliver"), `1136292748` (5★ titled "Crashing non stop"), `3596145485` (1★, "Amazing app, unlimited habit list… One of my favourite apps"), `5223935659` (1★, "Easy and convenient"). Separately, **54 reviews rated 1–2★ contain genuine product praise** — the "I love this app, but" pattern that makes the low-star band worth reading rather than dismissing. All 37 mismatches are listed in §9.3.

# PART 5 — PAID-USER AND UPGRADE ANALYSIS

## 5.1 Who is identifiable as a payer

**172 reviews (3.91% of the corpus) contain first-person evidence of payment** — `mp_paid_premium` (149: "I subscribed", "I have the paid version", "my subscription renewed") plus `mp_paid_lifetime` (23: explicit lifetime/one-time purchase). These sets do not overlap.

| Segment | n | Mean ★ | 5/4/3/2/1 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Self-identified payers | **172** | **3.92** | 108/14/9/10/31 |
| Everyone else | 4,230 | 4.24 | 2949/436/205/187/453 |

**Payers rate the app lower than non-payers** (3.92 vs 4.24), and 41 payer reviews are 1–2★. This is not dissatisfaction with the product — it is dissatisfaction with what happens to them after paying (§5.3).

Payers are concentrated in recent eras: 7 in E1, 8 in E2, 31 in E3, **75 in E4, 51 in E5** — i.e. 4.09–5.37% of recent reviews versus 1.89% of E3. Either more reviewers are paying, or paid users became more likely to write. Both readings are consistent with the tightening free tier; the corpus cannot distinguish them, and **no conversion rate can be inferred from any of this**.

Geography of payers: US 104, GB 17, CA 9, DE 6, RU 3, IN 3, ES 3, NL 3, BR 2, CN 2, KR 2, IT 2, plus singletons. GB over-indexes sharply (6.51% of its reviews vs 3.91% globally).

## 5.2 What triggers a purchase — in buyers' own words

Ranked by frequency among the 172 payer reviews:

1. **Hitting the tracker cap** — the dominant mechanical trigger. Reviewers describe upgrading specifically to add more trackers (`3309224718` "I'll probably upgrade to the paid version soon to get more trackers"; `10413395300`; `12143126487`).
2. **Sustained free use first, then payment as endorsement** — 38 payer reviews carry `mp_worth_the_price`; many describe months or years of free use before upgrading (`7091572225`: "used the free plan to track goals until yesterday I finally decided to switch to Pro to support the developers"; `4142865254`).
3. **Cross-device sync** — the paid feature most often named as the reason (`4180510540`, `11901589230`).
4. **Supporting the developer** — 9 reviews state this as the primary motive (`mp_pay_to_support_dev`), typically after a support interaction.
5. **The goal-setting course/book** — 27 reviews arrive via the companion content, and several convert from it (`12948472060`, `14500682632`, `2049600866`).

**Would-be buyers blocked at the price** are a separate, measurable group: 43 reviews state an intention to pay that the current terms defeat (`mp_willing_to_pay`, mean 4.12★), and 58 explicitly say they would pay a **one-time** fee but not a subscription. `8219828649` is the clearest single statement of the lost-conversion mechanism: "I will pay for an app after I use it for a couple weeks and have grown to appreciate the app… You are not allowing a certain group of people try out your app."

## 5.3 Post-purchase failures — where paying customers get hurt

**84 of 172 payer reviews (48.8% — a segment rate, against a global count of 84) also report a problem.** Breakdown within payers:

| Problem | Payers affected | Global n |
|---|---|---|
| `mf_price_too_high` (paid, still objects to value) | 14 | 266 |
| `mf_billing_problem` (double charge, unrecognised purchase, reverted sub) | 12 | 23 |
| `ux_confusing_setup` | 10 | 118 |
| `neg_stated_churn` (paid, now leaving) | 10 | 105 |
| `rel_crash_wont_open` | 9 | 185 |
| `mf_refund_request` | 8 | 23 |
| `sup_no_response` | 8 | 29 |
| `fr_better_reports` | 7 | 76 |
| `rel_sync_fail` | 6 | 48 |
| `rel_crash_on_action` | 6 | 82 |

**Billing is disproportionately a payer problem by definition, and it is the fastest-growing monetization fault in the corpus**: `mf_billing_problem` runs 1 review in E2, 4 in E3, 6 in E4 and **12 in E5 (1.44%)**. Its texts are specific and serious: charged $39.99 instead of the agreed $4.99 (`8374607922`); charged the full year at the *start* of a 7-day trial (`12011239688`); charged twice after cancelling before the trial ended, blocking other App Store purchases (`14509426891`, the final review in the corpus); "there is another active subscriber using the same receipt" (`8191839172`); paid subscription not recognised after an update (`4465653992`, `4724881241`, `7543156337`).

**The support trap compounds it.** Support is reachable only in-app, so a payer whose app will not open has no route: `9696594665`, `10162216990`, `11302947469`, `12211893087`, `13576643207`, `9559447782`. Several of these are one-star reviews written explicitly as help requests.

## 5.4 Refunds, cancellations and churn

- **23 refund requests** (`mf_refund_request`, mean **1.04★** — the lowest-mean theme in the corpus with n>10). Concentrated in 2014–2015 (paid-app buyers who felt the product failed) and 2019+ (trial and auto-renew disputes).
- **4 cancellation-difficulty reviews** (`mf_cancel_difficulty`) — below the reporting threshold on its own, retained because it is a compliance-adjacent complaint: `1304732873`, `1410463210`, `4325565920`, `14509426891`.
- **105 stated-churn reviews** (`neg_stated_churn`, 2.39%, mean 1.79★). Era rates: 2.34% E1, **9.74% E2**, 0.98% E3, 2.41% E4, 2.41% E5. Churn statements are running at roughly 2.4% of reviews and are no longer driven by crashes — in E4/E5 they pair most often with `mf_free_cap_too_low` and `neg_competitor_named`.
- **105 reviews name a competitor** as cheaper or better (`neg_competitor_named`). The names recur: Streaks, Habitify, Productive, Way of Life, Done, HabitBull, Habit, Loop, TickTick. The comparison is almost always about *price model*, not capability — reviewers concede Strides is more powerful and leave anyway (`3017109535`, `12023341090`, `4220820733`).

## 5.5 Barriers to upgrading — people who could have paid and did not

Four distinct barriers, each separately counted:

1. **Subscription as a model** — 48 reviews reject recurring billing in principle regardless of amount (`mf_subscription_objection`).
2. **One-time preference** — 58 reviews would pay once (`mf_want_one_time_purchase`); several name a figure, typically $10–$20.
3. **Lifetime price** — 28 reviews accept the model but reject the lifetime figure ($79.99–$149.99 as quoted), calling it disproportionate for a tracker (`mf_lifetime_price_too_high`).
4. **Cannot evaluate before paying** — the 48 `mf_paywall_not_disclosed` reviews plus the cap complaints: these users never reached a purchase decision because the free surface was too small to form one.

Barriers 1–3 are preference; barrier 4 is fixable product design, and it is the one that is growing.

## 5.6 Competitive position as reviewers describe it

Reviewers who benchmark (453 `cp_best_of_many`, plus 105 `neg_competitor_named`) describe a consistent trade: **Strides wins on tracking flexibility and loses on price model**. `3017109535` states it exactly — "Stride is a decent product but free version is useless which limits to only 7 habits… I eventually settled with Habitify which costs one time $10 and has comparable features." `4220820733` argues the same at length and stays hostile to the price while praising the product.

The corpus contains no evidence of a capability gap that is losing users. It contains repeated evidence of a *packaging* gap that is.

| XK (`xk`) | 1 | 0.02% | 5.00 | 1/0/0/0/0 | 0 | 2026-02 – 2026-02 | limited evidence |

## 6.2 United States — n = 2,485 (56.45%), mean 4.24

The US *is* the global picture, so the useful question is where it differs. Over-indexing (pp vs global): `cp_behaviour_change` +1.5 (189, 7.61%), `cp_accountability` +1.0 (88), `cp_visual_progress` +1.0 (160), `cp_reminders` +0.8 (146), `cp_streak_motivation` +0.7 (99), `cp_flexible_tracker_types` +0.7 (290). Under-indexing: `cp_design_aesthetic` −1.1, `meta_low_information` −1.0, `mf_price_too_high` −0.9.

**American reviewers talk about outcomes and accountability more than anyone else, and about aesthetics less.** They also supply 104 of the 172 payers (60.5%) and 71 of the 105 churn statements. Cap complaints run at 4.14% — *below* Canada (6.67%) and Germany's price rate, so the cap grievance is not a US-specific phenomenon.

## 6.3 Canada — n = 285 (6.47%), mean 4.16

The most price-sensitive eligible storefront. `mf_price_too_high` 27 (**9.47%**, +3.4pp vs global, high-priority) and `mf_free_cap_too_low` 19 (6.67%, +2.1pp). `neg_stated_churn` 11 (3.86%, +1.5pp) and `neg_competitor_named` 8 at mean **1.38★**. Praise is normal-to-strong (`cp_simplicity` 21.40%), so this is a pure packaging objection: Canadians like the app and resent the deal more than anyone else. Only 9 payers (3.16%, below the 3.91% global rate).

## 6.4 United Kingdom — n = 261 (5.93%), mean 4.13

The **most paid-engaged** storefront: 17 payers, **6.51%** of its reviews vs 3.91% globally (+2.6pp). Also over-indexes on `cp_simplicity` (22.22%), `cp_flexible_tracker_types` (13.79%), `cp_best_of_many` (13.03%), `cp_dev_listens` (4.21%, +1.9pp) and `seg_long_term_user` (4.98%, +1.9pp). Yet `mf_price_too_high` is 8.81% (+2.8pp) and `mf_free_cap_too_low` reviews average **1.73★**. The UK pattern is an engaged, paying, long-tenured base that is nonetheless vocal about price — the most valuable cohort to protect.

## 6.5 Australia — n = 124 (2.82%), mean 3.99

The lowest-rated eligible storefront. Over-indexes on `cp_dev_listens` (6.45%, +4.1pp — the highest anywhere), `cp_simplicity` (22.58%), `cp_flexible_tracker_types` (14.52%), `cp_bad_habit_tracking` (5.65%, +3.3pp) and `seg_fitness_weight` (4.84%). Against that: `mf_price_too_high` 9.68% (+3.6pp), `rel_crash_wont_open` 6.45% (+2.3pp), `neg_competitor_named` 4.84% at mean 1.83★, and `rel_data_loss` 3.23% (+1.9pp). Australia has both the most developer-appreciative reviewers and the most competitor-aware ones.

## 6.6 Germany — n = 119 (2.70%), mean 3.96

Distinctive on **privacy and model objections rather than amount**: `mf_forced_account_signup` 6 (**5.04%, +4.2pp** — the strongest over-index of any theme in any eligible storefront) and `mf_subscription_objection` 5 (4.20%, +3.1pp), alongside `mf_price_too_high` 13 (10.92%, +4.9pp). One 2016 review invokes German consumer law over the forced-sync conversion (`1314723695`). Also over-indexes on `cp_design_aesthetic` (8.40%) and `cp_support_responsive` (5.04%). Germany is where the *terms* are contested, not the utility.

## 6.7 Brazil, Russia, China, India — n = 88 / 79 / 73 / 71

- **Brazil (88, mean 4.59)** — the highest-rated eligible storefront and the least analytical: `cp_generic_positive` 34.09% (+14.6pp) and `meta_low_information` 21.59% (+12.1pp). `cp_best_of_many` 19.32% (+9.0pp) is real signal — Brazilians benchmark and prefer Strides — but the storefront's text volume per review is the lowest of the nine.
- **Russia (79, mean 4.14)** — `ux_no_localization` 8 (**10.13%, +9.2pp**). Russian-language requests span 2013 to 2017 and are the single clearest localization signal in the corpus. Also `cp_design_aesthetic` 10.13% and `cp_best_of_many` 17.72%.
- **China (73, mean 4.08)** — the sharpest split: `ux_no_localization` 9 (**12.33%, +11.4pp**) and `rel_crash_wont_open` 9 (**12.33%, +8.1pp**) against `cp_simplicity` at mean **5.00★**. A 2022 review celebrates Chinese localization arriving (`8249750576`). Price complaints are 10.96% and include the corpus's most vivid cost-of-living framing (`4715636334`: a lifetime plan priced near a quarter of a second-hand iPhone).
- **India (71, mean 4.42)** — `cp_flexible_tracker_types` 16.90% (+5.9pp), `mp_free_enough` 7.04%, and `rel_frozen_unresponsive` 4.23% (+3.3pp). Strongly positive, free-tier-satisfied, and sensitive to the 2020 freeze bug.

## 6.8 High-spend market group

**Defined as:** US, JP, GB, DE, CA, AU, FR, KR, CN — the nine markets that consistently lead Apple's published App Store consumer-spend rankings. All nine are present in the corpus. Membership is defined from that external ranking, **not** from this corpus's review volume; where the two groups differ is itself informative.

**n = 3,425 (77.81%), mean 4.21** vs rest-of-world 977 at **4.29**.

| Theme | High-spend | Rest of world |
|---|---|---|
| `mf_price_too_high` | 218 (6.36%) | 48 (4.91%) |
| `mf_want_one_time_purchase` | 50 (1.46%) | 8 (0.82%) |
| `mf_paid_then_subscription` | 65 (1.90%) | 5 (0.51%) |
| `mf_free_cap_too_low` | 149 (4.35%) | 50 (5.12%) |
| `cp_flexible_tracker_types` | 401 (11.71%) | 84 (8.60%) |
| `cp_support_responsive` | 115 (3.36%) | 12 (1.23%) |
| `ux_no_localization` | 18 (0.53%) | 21 (2.15%) |
| Payers | **142 (4.15%)** | 30 (3.07%) |

High-spend markets contain **82.6% of identified payers**, complain about price and the subscription model more, and complain about the free cap *less*. Rest-of-world reviewers are more likely to hit the cap complaint and four times more likely to raise localization.

## 6.9 High-review-volume market group

**Defined from this corpus** as the nine storefronts with ≥50 reviews: US, CA, GB, AU, DE, BR, RU, CN, IN. This is a **review-volume proxy, disclosed as such — it is not downloads and must not be read as such.** It differs from the high-spend group by swapping JP, KR and FR out for BR, RU and IN.

**n = 3,585 (81.44%), mean 4.22** vs rest 817 at 4.27. Payers 148 (4.13%) vs 24 (2.94%). The substitution matters in one direction only: the three swapped-in markets (BR, RU, IN) raise localization complaints at 2.33% versus 0.56% inside the group, and contribute proportionally fewer payers.

**Japan and Korea are absent from the eligible set** (18 and 24 reviews) despite being high-spend markets — a notable contrast with other apps in this series, where those storefronts dominate. Both are positive where present (JP 4.33, KR 4.54) and both appear only sporadically. This is a genuine distribution gap, not a sentiment problem, and it is flagged as limited evidence.

## 6.10 Global versus country comparison

| Theme | Global | US | CA | GB | AU | DE | BR | RU | CN | IN |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `cp_simplicity` | 18.56% | 18.39% | 21.40% | 22.22% | 22.58% | 21.01% | 13.64% | 6.33% | 17.81% | 15.49% |
| `cp_flexible_tracker_types` | 11.02% | 11.67% | 10.18% | 13.79% | 14.52% | 13.45% | 6.82% | 8.86% | 9.59% | 16.90% |
| `cp_behaviour_change` | 6.07% | 7.61% | 3.86% | 7.66% | 4.03% | — | 2.27% | 6.33% | — | 5.63% |
| `mf_price_too_high` | 6.04% | 5.19% | **9.47%** | 8.81% | **9.68%** | **10.92%** | 4.55% | 6.33% | **10.96%** | 2.82% |
| `mf_free_cap_too_low` | 4.52% | 4.14% | 6.67% | 4.21% | 4.84% | 4.20% | 5.68% | 2.53% | — | — |
| `rel_crash_wont_open` | 4.20% | 3.90% | 3.51% | 4.21% | 6.45% | 6.72% | — | 6.33% | **12.33%** | 5.63% |
| `ux_no_localization` | 0.89% | — | — | — | — | — | 2.27% | **10.13%** | **12.33%** | — |
| Payers | 3.91% | 4.19% | 3.16% | **6.51%** | 1.61% | 5.04% | 2.27% | 3.80% | 2.74% | 4.23% |

(— = below 2% in that storefront.) **Praise is remarkably uniform; grievance is local.** Simplicity and tracker flexibility land within a few points everywhere, while price sensitivity clusters in CA/AU/DE/CN and localization in RU/CN.

## 6.11 Sub-50 storefronts — limited evidence only

81 storefronts hold 817 reviews (18.56%). They are included in every global figure and carry **no standalone claims**. Two observations are recorded as limited evidence because they are directionally consistent and cheap to verify:

- **Indonesia (13 reviews, mean 3.08)** is the lowest-rated storefront with n>10, driven by two long multilingual 1★ reviews about sync failure and ignored feedback (`11112816965`, `11918628097`). Limited evidence.
- **Ireland (16, mean 4.88), Taiwan (14, 4.79), Malaysia (19, 4.58)** sit at the top. All below threshold. Limited evidence.

# PART 7 — TIME-TREND ANALYSIS

## 7.1 Method

Five eras, cut at **documented product events visible in the review texts themselves**, not at arbitrary intervals:

| Era | Window and what changed | n | Mean ★ | 1–2★ share | Monetization-friction rate | Reliability rate |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| E1 | Jul 2013–Dec 2014 (paid app, one-time purchase) | 342 | 3.78 | 74 (21.6%) | 2.3% | 32.2% |
| E2 | Jan–Sep 2015 (v3.0 subscription + forced account) | 308 | 2.21 | 210 (68.2%) | 50.0% | 52.6% |
| E3 | Oct 2015–Jun 2018 (freemium relaunch, ~10 free trackers) | 1636 | 4.62 | 101 (6.2%) | 7.8% | 6.3% |
| E4 | Jul 2018–Dec 2021 (free cap tightened 10→7→3) | 1285 | 4.32 | 164 (12.8%) | 14.4% | 10.8% |
| E5 | Jan 2022–Sep 2026 (3-tracker free tier, Mac app, Health/lifetime) | 831 | 4.23 | 132 (15.9%) | 17.2% | 8.3% |

E1/E2 boundary: 19 January 2015, the v3.0 subscription release — reviewers date it themselves. E2/E3: October 2015, when reviews begin announcing a free unlimited tier (`1273838707`, 19 Oct 2015). E3/E4: mid-2018, when reviewers first report the free cap dropping from 10 to 7 (`2957558274`, 24 Jul 2018). E4/E5: January 2022, a clean calendar boundary after which the 3-tracker cap, the Mac app and Health integration dominate. Sensitivity: moving any boundary by ±2 months changes no era mean by more than 0.08★.

Also reported: per-year rates (§1.4), per-month rates through the 2015 crisis, and five incident windows.

## 7.2 Trend 1 — The 2015 subscription collapse and recovery · **high confidence**

The corpus's defining event, visible at monthly resolution:

| Month | n | Mean ★ | 1–2★ | "paid then sub" | price | forced account | crash |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2014-12 | 13 | 2.31 | 61.5% | 0 | 0 | 0 | 5 |
| **2015-01** | **125** | **2.50** | **60.0%** | **14** | **14** | **13** | **52** |
| **2015-02** | 56 | **1.66** | **83.9%** | 12 | 10 | 1 | 27 |
| 2015-03 | 37 | 2.46 | 64.9% | 6 | 9 | 1 | 6 |
| 2015-04 | 30 | 2.10 | 73.3% | 11 | 9 | 2 | 3 |
| 2015-06 | 13 | 2.08 | 76.9% | 7 | 2 | 3 | 1 |
| 2015-09 | 10 | 2.10 | 70.0% | 4 | 3 | 0 | 0 |
| **2015-10** | 21 | **4.14** | **19.0%** | 3 | 2 | 2 | 0 |
| 2015-12 | 30 | 4.37 | 13.3% | 0 | 2 | 0 | 0 |

The January 2015 window (19 Jan – 30 Sep, n=272) runs **mean 2.06★ with 73.2% 1–2★**. Two failures compounded: the commercial change (60 `mf_paid_then_subscription`, 25 `mf_forced_account_signup`, 14 `mf_grandfather_broken`) and a simultaneously broken release (81 crash-on-launch, 25 data-loss, 20 duplicate-tracker reviews). Reviewers explicitly link them — they were asked to start paying for an app that had just stopped working.

**The recovery in October 2015 is equally abrupt** and coincides with reviews announcing a free, unlimited tier. From 2.10★ in September to 4.14★ in October to a 4.62★ era average. `1273838707`, `1274461925` and `1274551764` all thank the developer for reversing course; `1274461925` is explicit: "I was very upset when I realised I could no longer access my data unless I paid… I appreciate the ability to continue using the app for free."

## 7.3 Trend 2 — The free cap is the new grievance, and it is still rising · **high confidence**

`mf_free_cap_too_low` by era: **0.00% → 0.32% → 1.59% → 7.00% → 9.87%**. In the last 24 months it is **12.1%** of all reviews. No other negative theme in this corpus is still climbing.

The cap number reviewers report *falls* over time — 10 (2016–2018), 7 (2018–2019), 5–6 (2019–2020), 3 (2020–2026), with a handful claiming 2 or 1 — and the complaints move with it. `mf_free_cap_reduced` (23 reviews) is the specific, most damaging variant: users who deleted a tracker to make room and found the slot gone. Mean 2.22★.

Read against `mp_free_enough`, which falls from 7.82% of E3 to 4.81% of E5, the pattern is coherent: as the free tier shrank, satisfaction with it fell and complaints about it rose, roughly in proportion.

## 7.4 Trend 3 — Price complaints fell as the product matured · **high confidence**

`mf_price_too_high`: 0.58% (E1) → **21.43% (E2)** → 4.89% (E3) → 6.15% (E4) → 4.69% (E5). The E2 spike is the conversion; the long decline after it is real. `mf_paid_then_subscription` is effectively extinct after 2016 (3 reviews in E5, all returning users). **Strides' pricing anger has migrated from "too expensive" to "I can't try it."**

## 7.5 Trend 4 — Reliability was fixed, and stayed fixed · **high confidence**

Crash union (`rel_crash_wont_open` + `rel_crash_on_action` + `rel_frozen_unresponsive`): 76 (E1, 22.2% of era) → 115 (E2, 37.3%) → 31 (E3, 1.9%) → 52 (E4, 4.0%) → **24 (E5, 2.9%)**. The whole Reliability family drops from 52.6% of E2 to 8.3% of E5.

Two residual pockets. `rel_frozen_unresponsive` spikes in 2020 (25 reviews in E4 — an iOS 13/14-era freeze reported from many storefronts: `5945320813`, `6081780984`, `6121865161`, `6282256453`, `6358108341`, `6534730756`) and then vanishes. And `rel_sync_fail` never improves: 11/12/12/13 across E2–E5, making it the most common reliability complaint in the current era (`13458071780`, `14042521389`, `11165592973`).

## 7.6 Trend 5 — The 2021 logging redesign · **medium-high confidence**

In May–August 2021 (n=78, mean 3.86★, 28.2% 1–2★) ten reviews complain that a redesign of history logging multiplied the taps needed to back-fill days: `7382413590` ("Latest update forces you to double click for every single activity"), `7390981774`, `7397956611`, `7412691840`, `7447645065`, `7585378241`, `7623220041`, `13025306978` (2025, "turning a 10 second daily update to about 60"). These come disproportionately from long-term users (7 of 78 reviews in the window carry `seg_long_term_user`) and payers (6).

The developer responded: `7521539649` records the one-click behaviour being restored in charts, and `7540012549` describes the reinstatement explicitly. `ux_too_many_taps` falls to 0.24% in E5. **The episode is resolved but it cost ratings from the most loyal cohort — the standing lesson is that log-entry friction is uniquely expensive for this product.**

## 7.7 Trend 6 — The upgrade nag arrived in 2024 · **medium confidence, small n**

`mf_upsell_nag` is 13 reviews total, but its distribution is informative: 2 in 2015, 5 across 2017–2023, and **6 in the three months from July 2024** (`11486930156`, `11606910612`, `11624276095`, `11652177792`, `11720024657`, `11757185028`). All describe a full-screen or post-log upgrade prompt that did not previously exist, and several are long-term free users explicitly stating the nag *reduced* their willingness to pay: "I might even consider paying money for the app. But I darn sure will not… as long as the nag screen is still in place" (`11757185028`). One reviewer reports it was subsequently softened (`11624276095`, updated to 4★).

Small sample, so this is flagged as medium confidence — but it is the only monetization mechanic in the corpus that users say actively reduced their intent to pay.

## 7.8 Trend 7 — The base is ageing, and it is becoming the loudest voice · **high confidence**

`seg_long_term_user` (explicit multi-year use): 0.00% (E1) → 0.65% (E2) → 1.41% (E3) → 3.19% (E4) → **8.54% (E5)**. In the last 24 months it is 7.8%. These reviewers report 5, 8, 10-year tenures and 500- to 3,497-day streaks (`13989739905`, `11672096169`, `13646840535`, `10571805250`).

They are also over-represented in the two most recent grievances: long-term users are 7 of the 2021 tap-friction reviews and appear repeatedly in cap-reduction and nag-screen complaints. **Strides' most loyal cohort is now its most exposed to packaging changes**, because they are the ones who remember the earlier terms.

## 7.9 Trend 8 — Platform expansion landed well · **medium-high confidence**

`cp_multi_platform_sync` recovers from 0.78% (E4) to **3.13% (E5)** and `cp_health_shortcuts` rises 0.00% → 0.31% → **1.56%**, tracking the Mac app (Mar 2022: `8407086509`, `8409343866`, `9053449079`), Apple Health automation and Siri Shortcuts (`9541417489`, `10212102204`, `11416073129`), and the Watch app (`cp_watch_app` 1.08% of E5). Against that, 17 reviews still want the discontinued **web app** back or an Android build (`fr_web_or_other_platform`, rising to 0.84% of E5) — Windows users in particular lost access (`12482915746`, `12544429258`).

## 7.10 Trend 9 — What did not change in eleven years

- **Simplicity praise** is 16–22% in every era. It has never been the problem.
- **`ux_confusing_setup`** sits at 2–3% in E1, E3, E4 and E5 — a decade of onboarding friction that no redesign has removed, now 2.89% of E5.
- **Reminders** are praised (224) and faulted (78) at a stable ratio throughout.
- **No ads, ever.** 33 praise it; zero complain.
- **Support quality** holds: `cp_support_responsive` is 3.51% of E1 and 3.61% of E5, with `sup_fix_acknowledged` steady around 1.2%.

---

# PART 8 — PRODUCT IMPLICATIONS

Every recommendation names the findings it rests on. "Immediate" means the corpus already establishes both the problem and its cost; "experiment" means the corpus establishes the problem but not the remedy; "research" means the corpus raises the question and cannot answer it.

## 8.1 Immediate fixes — evidence is unambiguous

1. **Disclose the free-tracker limit before setup, on the store listing and at first run.** 48 reviews (`mf_paywall_not_disclosed`, mean **1.65★**, rising to 2.05% of E5) describe investing setup effort and only then meeting the wall. This is the cheapest 1★ in the corpus to eliminate: it costs one line of copy and removes a grievance that is *about the surprise*, not the price. [§3.4.1, §5.5]
2. **Never reduce an existing user's free capacity again, and restore slots freed by deletion.** 23 reviews (`mf_free_cap_reduced`, mean **2.22★**) describe deleting a tracker and finding the slot gone, or the cap shrinking under a history they had already built. This reads as retroactive punishment and it lands on the longest-tenured users. [§3.4.1, §7.3, §7.8]
3. **Give support a route that works when the app does not.** 14 reviews (`sup_only_reachable_in_app`, 1.08% of E5 and growing) are 1★ reviews written *as* support tickets because in-app help was unreachable. Publish a support email on the website and in the App Store listing. Nine of these are from payers. [§3.4.6, §5.3]
4. **Fix the billing edge cases.** 23 billing-fault reviews (`mf_billing_problem`), **12 of them in E5** — full-year charges at trial start, double charges after cancellation, purchases not recognised after update, "another active subscriber using the same receipt". These reviews average 2.3★ and several escalate to refund demands and App Store disputes. This is the fastest-growing monetization fault in the corpus. [§5.3, §5.4]
5. **Close out sync.** `rel_sync_fail` is the only reliability theme that never improved (11/12/12/13 across E2–E5) and is now the most common reliability complaint in the current era. [§3.4.4, §7.5]

## 8.2 Structural changes — high confidence in direction

6. **Raise the free tier to the point where the differentiator is demonstrable.** The product's moat is *multi-type tracking across several goals* (485 reviews, §3.3.2). A 3-tracker tier cannot show it. 211 reviews say so directly, the theme is the only growing negative in the corpus (12.1% of the last 24 months), and 43 reviewers state an intent to pay that the current terms defeat. The corpus does not prove what the right number is — it proves that the 2015–2018 era, with a large free tier, produced the **highest ratings (4.62★), the most free-tier gratitude (7.82%) and a growing payer base**. [§3.4.1, §5.2, §5.5, §7.3]
7. **Offer a cheap, narrow paid tier that only unlocks trackers.** 58 reviews want a one-time purchase and 48 reject subscriptions as a model; separately, 28 accept the model but reject the lifetime figure. Multiple reviewers propose exactly this split themselves — pay once for capacity, subscribe for sync/reports (`2678777187`, `7031612228`, `12919506637`). [§5.5]
8. **Remove or soften the post-log upgrade prompt.** 6 of the 13 `mf_upsell_nag` reviews arrived in three months of 2024, and they state plainly that the nag *reduced* willingness to pay (`11757185028`). One reviewer records it being softened and raised their rating (`11624276095`). The corpus contains no evidence the prompt converted anyone and direct evidence it repelled people. [§7.7]
9. **Treat log-entry friction as sacred.** The 2021 history redesign cost ten reviews in three months from long-tenured, paying users, and its reversal was welcomed by name (`7521539649`, `7540012549`). Simplicity of logging is praised in 817 reviews; it is the product's most load-bearing quality. [§3.3.1, §7.6]
10. **Ship weekday scheduling and a skip/pause mode.** 21 reviews (`ux_weekday_scheduling`) describe weekly goals that still demand daily answers and weekend "misses" for weekday habits; 13 (`ux_no_skip_or_pause`) want vacation/illness handling that does not destroy a streak or an average. Both are scoring-correctness complaints from otherwise-happy users, sustained across all five eras. [§3.4.5]

## 8.3 What not to change

- **Do not add ads.** 33 reviews praise their absence; zero complain about them. Two reviewers propose ads over paywalls — that is not endorsement of ads, it is hostility to the cap.
- **Do not simplify away the four tracker types.** They are the single most-cited reason people choose Strides over rivals (485 reviews) and the reason benchmarking reviewers return (453).
- **Do not redesign the interface for novelty.** 62 reviews call the UI dated and 14 explicitly praise its plainness ("does not include 'cute' graphics", `6670709795`). The dated-UI complaint is real but small and stable (1.68% of E5); the tap-count lesson of §7.6 is the larger risk.
- **Do not lose the support culture.** 127 reviews praise it, many by name; it demonstrably converts 1★ reviewers into 5★ reviewers (`1811801602`, `2858976575`, `7540012549`; see §9.3).

## 8.4 Experiments worth running

- **A larger free tier, measured on paid conversion rather than on rating.** The E3 period pairs the largest free tier with the highest ratings *and* a growing payer share — but that is correlation across eras with many other changes, and the corpus cannot isolate it.
- **A tracker-only paid tier at a low one-time price**, tested against the 58 explicit one-time requesters' stated range ($10–$20).
- **Onboarding that teaches goal-type selection.** `ux_confusing_setup` has been stuck at 2–3% for a decade (118 reviews) and concentrates on choosing between Habit/Target/Average/Project. The goal-setting course already exists and is credited by 27 reviewers — surface it at first run.
- **Reports/trends as the next paid feature.** 76 reviews request it, from satisfied users (mean 4.07★), and it is still open as of August 2026.

## 8.5 Research questions this corpus raises but cannot answer

1. **What is the actual free-tier limit today, and when did each change ship?** Reviewers report 10/8/7/6/5/4/3/2/1 inconsistently, sometimes within the same month. Only internal release data can date this.
2. **Did the tightening cap raise revenue?** The corpus shows the rating and sentiment cost. It cannot see conversions, and the payer-share rise in E4/E5 is equally explicable by composition change.
3. **Why are Japan and Korea nearly absent** (18 and 24 reviews) despite being high-spend markets where this app's category performs well?
4. **How large is the prompt-driven share of the 379 five-star low-information reviews?**
5. **Did the 2024 upgrade prompt convert anyone?** Seven reviews say it repelled them; the corpus cannot see the other side of that ledger.

---

# PART 9 — EVIDENCE APPENDIX

## 9.1 Theme definitions and boundary rules

117 themes in 11 families. Each definition is the rule actually applied while reading; counts are global (N = 4,402).

### Core praise (24 themes)

| Theme | n | Assigned when |
|---|---|---|
| `cp_generic_positive` | 858 | Positive with no specific product reason given |
| `cp_simplicity` | 817 | Praises simplicity, ease of use, clean uncluttered interface |
| `cp_flexible_tracker_types` | 485 | Praises the four tracker types / tracking numbers, averages, targets, not just yes-no |
| `cp_best_of_many` | 453 | Tried many trackers (or paper/spreadsheet); this is the best / came back to it |
| `cp_behaviour_change` | 267 | Reports a real outcome: weight lost, quit smoking, sober, flossing, business result |
| `cp_visual_progress` | 241 | Charts, graphs, progress bars, calendar view make progress visible |
| `cp_reminders` | 224 | Praises reminders, multiple alerts, custom reminder text |
| `cp_design_aesthetic` | 212 | Praises the look: beautiful, elegant, well designed |
| `cp_customization` | 167 | Praises how configurable/customisable goals and reminders are |

### Monetization friction (16 themes)

| Theme | n | Assigned when |
|---|---|---|
| `mf_price_too_high` | 266 | Subscription price is too high for what the app does |
| `mf_free_cap_too_low` | 199 | The free tier's tracker/habit cap is too low to be useful |
| `mf_paid_then_subscription` | 70 | Bought the app, then was required to subscribe to keep using it |
| `mf_want_one_time_purchase` | 58 | Asks for a one-off purchase instead of a subscription |
| `mf_paywall_not_disclosed` | 48 | Limit or paywall not disclosed before download / after setup work |
| `mf_subscription_objection` | 48 | Objects to the subscription model in principle ('I won't rent an app') |
| `mf_forced_account_signup` | 39 | Objects to being forced to create an account or go online to use the app |
| `mf_lifetime_price_too_high` | 28 | The one-time / lifetime price specifically is too high |
| `mf_trial_terms` | 26 | Trial length, auto-renewal or trial charge not clear or not honoured |
| `mf_billing_problem` | 23 | Wrong charge, double charge, purchase not recognised, subscription reverted |
| `mf_free_cap_reduced` | 23 | Says the free cap was cut over time (10 -> 7 -> 3), or deleting one frees no slot |
| `mf_refund_request` | 23 | Explicitly asks for or was denied a refund |
| `mf_feature_removed_free` | 17 | A feature was taken out of the free tier (calendar, averages, web, notes) |
| `mf_grandfather_broken` | 14 | Promised free-for-life / grandfathered access was not honoured |
| `mf_upsell_nag` | 13 | Upgrade pop-up or nag screen after logging / on launch |
| `mf_cancel_difficulty` | 4 | Cannot find or complete cancellation of the subscription |

### Meta (4 themes)

| Theme | n | Assigned when |
|---|---|---|
| `meta_low_information` | 416 | No product content at all (single word, emoji, gibberish) |
| `meta_rating_text_mismatch` | 37 | Star rating contradicts the text |
| `meta_duplicate_text` | 3 | Substantially duplicates another review in the corpus |
| `meta_review_prompt` | 3 | Mentions having been asked to leave the review |

**Boundary rules applied consistently:**

- `mf_price_too_high` vs `mf_free_cap_too_low` — the first objects to the *amount*, the second to *how little you get free*. Reviews making both arguments carry both (74 do).
- `mf_paid_then_subscription` requires the reviewer to state they had *bought* the app before the change. Generic subscription dislike is `mf_subscription_objection`.
- `cp_simplicity` requires a stated reason (easy, clean, fast, uncluttered). Bare enthusiasm is `cp_generic_positive`.
- `meta_low_information` is assigned only when a review contains no product content whatsoever; it co-occurs with `cp_generic_positive` when the review is positive but contentless.
- `rel_crash_wont_open` (app terminates at/near launch) vs `rel_crash_on_action` (crash on a named action) vs `rel_frozen_unresponsive` (opens, taps do nothing). Disjoint by rule.
- `mp_paid_premium`/`mp_paid_lifetime` require first-person purchase evidence, not intent. Intent is `mp_willing_to_pay`.
- `fr_*` themes describe capabilities that do not exist; `rel_*` describe capabilities that exist and fail; `ux_confusing_setup` describes capabilities that exist and were not found.

## 9.2 Every quantified composite finding, in one table

| Finding | n | % of 4,402 | Mean ★ | Signal | Section |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Any praise (core + monetization praise + fix acknowledged) | 3,296 | 74.88% | 4.82 | high-priority | §3.2 |
| Core praise family | 3,186 | 72.38% | 4.84 | high-priority | §3.2 |
| Monetization friction family | 618 | 14.04% | 2.53 | high-priority | §3.2 |
| Reliability family | 583 | 13.24% | 2.40 | high-priority | §3.2 |
| UX friction family | 346 | 7.86% | 3.35 | high-priority | §3.2 |
| Feature requests family | 321 | 7.29% | 4.07 | high-priority | §3.5 |
| Product-value criticism family | 250 | 5.68% | 2.28 | high-priority | §3.2 |
| Purchase evidence (payers) | 172 | 3.91% | 3.92 | very strong | §5.1 |
| Support family | 95 | 2.16% | 3.43 | meaningful | §3.4.6 |
| **Price union** (`mf_price_too_high` + one-time + subscription-objection + lifetime-price) | 316 | 7.18% | 2.74 | high-priority | §3.4.2 |
| **Crash union** (won't-open + on-action + frozen) | 298 | 6.77% | 2.13 | high-priority | §3.4.4 |
| **Free-cap union** (`too_low` + `reduced` + `raise_free_cap`) | 211 | 4.79% | 2.68 | very strong | §3.4.1 |
| **2015 conversion union** (paid-then-sub + forced-account + grandfather-broken) | 115 | 2.61% | 1.57 | meaningful | §3.4.3 |
| Sync failure + data loss | 103 | 2.34% | 2.14 | meaningful | §3.4.4 |
| Payers who also report a problem | 84 | 1.91% (48.8% of payers) | 2.83 | meaningful | §5.3 |
| Support failure (no response + in-app-only) | 40 | 0.91% | 1.57 | emerging | §3.4.6 |
| ADHD / mental-health context | 35 | 0.80% | 4.40 | emerging | §9.3 |
| Lifetime purchasers | 23 | 0.52% | 4.52 | emerging | §5.1 |
| Refund requests | 23 | 0.52% | 1.04 | emerging | §5.4 |
| Rating-vs-text contradictions | 37 | 0.84% | — | weak | §4.7 |
| Free-cap complaints, last 24 months | 32 | 12.5% of 256 | 2.41 | segment rate | §7.3 |

**Segment rates** (denominator is the segment, never the corpus): payers-with-a-problem 48.8% of 172; free-cap complaints 12.1% of the last 24 months' 256 reviews; 1–2★ reviews attributable to monetization friction 51.0% of 681; to reliability 49.5% of 681.

## 9.4 Counting rules, denominators and reconciliation summary

- **Denominator.** Unless a table says otherwise, every percentage is out of **4,402**. Country percentages use that country's total. Segment rates state their own denominator and are labelled.
- **Non-exclusivity.** Themes overlap by design; the 4,402 reviews carry 8,804 theme assignments, a mean of 2.00 each. Family rows are *unions* — a review with four reliability themes counts once in the Reliability family row.
- **Means.** "Mean ★" is the arithmetic mean of `rating` over the reviews carrying that theme. It measures the company a theme keeps, not its severity.
- **Signal labels.** Applied to global shares and, separately, to each of the nine eligible storefronts using that storefront's denominator (§6). Sub-50 storefronts get no labels.
- **Eras.** Assigned by review publication date (§7.1). A review written in 2026 about 2015 behaviour counts in E5.
- **Reconciliation.** 4,402 records in, 4,402 read, 4,402 classified, 0 unassigned, 0 duplicates, 0 unknown IDs. Main file, 90 country files, `manifest.json` and `_state.json` agree exactly on every count, every rating and every per-country total (§1.3).

## 9.5 Provenance of this analysis

Every review was read individually, in original language, in date order, in 45 chunks of 100. Themes were assigned while reading and recorded against the working-file index; `48-build-classification.py` resolved index → `review_id` from `reviews.jsonl` deterministically, so no ID was transcribed by hand. `48-validate.py` then confirmed the map against the source. All tables in §3.1, §3.2, §4.6, §6.1, §7.1, §9.1, §9.7 and §9.8 are machine-generated from that map by `48-tables.py`; the prose was written against `48-aggregate-out.txt` and `48-trends-out.txt`. `48-verify-report.py` re-checks the finished file: every cited ID exists, every index row matches the source record and the map, every master-table row matches recomputed counts, every theme ID list matches the map.

Known error risk: classification judgement on short or ambiguous reviews, which defaults to neutral themes and therefore understates rather than overstates substantive findings (§1.5).

## 9.6 External sources used

| Source | Accessed | Used for |
|---|---|---|
| App Store listing, Strides (ID 672401817) | 12 Sep 2026 | Current price tiers, version 19.13, 4.8★/~19,000 ratings, 3-tracker free tier, language list, iOS 17 minimum (§2.3) |
| stridesapp.com | 12 Sep 2026 | Four tracker types, platform support, Android waitlist (§2.3) |

Both are cited only in §2.3 and in the warning about written-vs-all ratings. No finding in §3–§7 depends on either.
